review-of-the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot — delivered report

Cited from /home/ITER/mcintos/.config/reckon/crew/reports/reckon/s22-coord/review-of-the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot/report.md.

# Read-back — `reckon:the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot` §2

Node: `review-of-the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot`
Plan revision read: `4184c771c` (plan version 4), section §2.
Role: read-back before release. This is a restatement, not a critique of the
design. Where a sentence carries two readings I quote it verbatim and give each
reading; I do not choose between them.

---

## 1. The plan's intent, in my own words

Every coordinator prompt runs `reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py`. That
hook currently starts a fresh Python process and, in that process, re-derives
the entire fleet's state from scratch: it parses every plan, classifies every
live run pointer, reads whole worker event streams, and loads the whole run
ledger. Measured on 2026-10-01 that cold derivation took **31.4 s** for the S21
session, against a **30 s hook time limit** — and the limit had already been
crossed once before. The plan's argument is that no amount of tuning the
derivation reaches millisecond latency while the hook still performs the
derivation on every turn, because every term grows with the fleet.

The plan therefore moves the derivation out of the hook and into the process
that already runs continuously and already watches every pointer — the
per-project watch producer (`runs.watch`, started by `reckon crew watch
--ensure`). The producer publishes each session's obligations as a snapshot
file; the hook becomes a reader of that published file, so a turn costs
milliseconds (a stat plus a small JSON read) instead of a full re-derivation.
Section §4 additionally makes the derivation itself incremental (caches keyed by
file stat identity) so the producer's own recompute, the CLI, the MCP view and
`runs.drain` are all cheap. Section §5 adds call-count guards so the speed is
enforced deterministically rather than by wall-clock assertions.

## 2. Every decision, restated

1. **`where-derivation-runs` — "Which process derives and publishes
   obligations?"** Choice: **the per-project watch producer** (not each
   session's follower). Rationale in the plan: the producer is always on, keeps
   warm caches across sweeps, and already does the per-pointer classification;
   a session's follower only lives while that session's harness monitor is
   armed, and the monitor re-arms every 25 minutes, so a follower-published
   snapshot would go stale exactly in the gap between re-arms. Recorded
   `by: s22-coord`, `when: 2026-10-01`.

2. **`stale-snapshot-at-stop` — "When the stop hook finds no fresh snapshot,
   what does it do?"** Choice: **`bounded-derive`** (recorded `by: lead`,
   `when: 2026-10-01T13:02:22+00:00`). The stop hook, which enforces obligations
   by refusing a stop, does not depend on a live producer: when the snapshot is
   not fresh it derives inline under a time budget of about 5 s — which §4
   makes feasible by bringing a cold derivation under 2 s — and blocks only on
   that inline result. If the budget runs out first, it allows the stop and
   prints a line naming the stale snapshot, so a dead or outdated producer can
   never trap a coordinator into an uncloseable turn. The two rejected options
   were `warn-and-allow` (allow the stop with a line naming the stale snapshot,
   with no inline enforcement) and `block-until-producer` (refuse the stop until
   a producer is running again).

## 3. Every done-when, restated

- **§1** (status `done`, a measurement section): no done-when; it records the
  current cost — 31.4 s cold, 11.1 s warm same session, 9.3 s for `s22-coord`,
  16.9 s as the S21 session timed it under load 43 — and the cold profile
  (plan reads 19.7 s, pointer classification 8.4 s, meta parsing 3.0 s, ledger
  2.8 s).

- **§2 — the producer publishes.** Done when: (a) a pointer transition shows up
  in the affected session's snapshot within one producer sweep; (b) the
  snapshot's obligations equal `obligations()` computed at the same instant over
  a synthesised fleet — the parity case; and (c) a producer killed mid-write
  leaves the previous snapshot intact (atomic temp-file-plus-rename).

- **§3 — the hook reads, never derives.** Done when: (a) 20 consecutive cold
  invocations of the prompt hook on the real fleet give a p95 under 250 ms;
  (b) a test shows the hook's import surface excludes `reckon._plan_html`,
  `reckon._backends` and `reckon.ledger`; and (c) each not-fresh reason emits
  its line, with a control that makes the hook derive inline and fails the
  import-surface case.

- **§4 — the derivation becomes incremental.** Done when: `obligations()` for
  the largest live session runs under 2 s cold in a fresh process and under
  0.3 s on a repeat call, with a parity case showing the cached derivation
  returns the same obligations as the uncached one over a synthesised fleet of
  40 pointers and 200 plans.

- **§5 — guards.** Done when: on the synthesised fleet a hook invocation calls
  none of `read_state`, `parse_events`, `ledger.load` (each patched to raise);
  between two producer sweeps with one plan rewritten, `read_state` runs for
  that plan only; a pointer whose inputs did not change is not re-classified.
  Wall-clock budgets are recorded as evidence measurements, never asserted in
  the suite.

- **§6 — order of work.** No done-when; it fixes the sequence: §4 item 1 first,
  then §2 and §3 together, then §4 items 2 and 3; the stop path of §3 lands
  after §4 item 1.

---

## 4. Sentences with two or more readings

Quoted verbatim; readings labelled A/B.

1. **§2 — "After any sweep in which an input changed, it derives the obligations
   of each session that has a delivering follower"**
   - A: "an input" is any of the three triggers named below (pointer transition,
     ledger/plan-store change, floor tick) — i.e. this sentence is the general
     statement the three bullets enumerate.
   - B: "an input" is specifically a change to state the *producer reads*
     (pointer, ledger, plan store), with the pointer transition being a distinct
     trigger — and the floor tick is not an "input" at all, so the floor-tick
     bullet is an exception to this sentence rather than an instance of it.

2. **§2 — "it derives the obligations of each session that has a delivering
   follower"**
   - A: obligations are published only for sessions that currently have a live
     follower that is delivering traffic; a session with no follower gets no
     snapshot, and its hook path therefore always takes the not-fresh branch.
   - B: "delivering follower" is a harness/state attribute that persists on the
     session record regardless of live traffic, so nearly every active session
     gets a snapshot.

3. **§2 — "computed_at, and the event-stream offset the snapshot reflects"**
   - A: one offset, of the single event stream relevant to that session.
   - B: an offset per stream (the snapshot may reflect several streams), the
     singular "offset" being shorthand.

4. **§2 — "the producer's pid and the code stamp of the source it is running"**
   - A: "code stamp" is a commit sha of the checked-out source.
   - B: "code stamp" is any identity of the running source (sha, file mtime,
     or a dirty-tree marker), the test being only that §3 can compare it to the
     installed source's stamp.

5. **§2 — "a floor tick every 60 s, so obligations that become due by the
   passage of time, such as a stale promotable run, appear without any other
   event"**
   - A: every 60 s the producer recomputes the full obligations payload for
     every covered session (a time-driven full sweep).
   - B: the floor tick recomputes only the time-dependent part (the ages / due
     flags), leaving the change-driven part as computed by the other triggers.

6. **§2 — "the snapshot's obligations equal `obligations()` computed at the
   same instant on a synthesised fleet (a parity case)"**
   - A: the producer is frozen/driven deterministically so that a live
     `obligations()` call and the published snapshot are taken from the same
     fleet state, making the comparison exact.
   - B: the two are compared across the smallest observable interval, accepting
     that "the same instant" is only approximately achievable on a live
     producer.

7. **§2 — "a producer killed mid-write leaves the previous snapshot intact"**
   - A: "mid-write" means between writing the temporary file and the rename, so
     the case exercises the atomic-rename boundary directly.
   - B: "mid-write" means at any point during the write/rename sequence,
     including after the rename, in which case a kill can legitimately leave the
     new snapshot in place and the assertion must be scoped to the pre-rename
     window only.

8. **§3 — "The snapshot is fresh when the producer pid is alive"**
   - A: liveness is checked as pid-alive (e.g. `os.kill(pid, 0)` or a process
     probe), with the ordinary pid-reuse hazard accepted.
   - B: liveness means "the process that wrote this snapshot is still the
     process holding this pid", which requires a stronger check (start time or
     a lock) than bare pid aliveness.

9. **§3 — "A fresh snapshot is injected exactly as today, including the existing
   digest that suppresses an unchanged list"**
   - A: "injected" = the snapshot payload is placed into the hook's prompt
     context, and "as today" means the surrounding rendering is unchanged.
   - B: "injected" = fed through the existing injection function whose behavior
     (including the digest) is reused wholesale, so any change to that function
     is out of scope for §3.

10. **§3 — "The hook exits 0 in every case. It never imports the derivation
    modules"**
    - A: "the hook" here is the prompt hook, and "derivation modules" is exactly
      the three named in the done-when (`reckon._plan_html`, `reckon._backends`,
      `reckon.ledger`).
    - B: "the hook" covers both prompt and stop paths, and "derivation modules"
      is any module that derives obligations — which would put the stop path's
      inline derivation (the next paragraph) in direct tension with this
      sentence.

11. **§3 — "When the snapshot is not fresh it derives inline under a budget of
    about 5 s ... and blocks only on that result."**
    - A: "blocks only on that result" = the block/allow decision is taken
      solely from the inline derivation's result, never from anything else.
    - B: "blocks only on that result" = the stop is refused only when that
      result says work is outstanding (i.e. it always blocks on a true result,
      allows otherwise).

12. **§3 — "If the budget runs out it allows the stop with a line naming the
    stale snapshot, so a dead producer never traps a coordinator."**
    - A: "a dead producer" is literal (no producer running).
    - B: "a dead producer" is a shorthand for the whole not-fresh family —
      no producer, wrong-code producer, or merely old snapshot — all of which
      the sentence's guarantee must cover.

13. **§3 — "20 consecutive cold invocations of the prompt hook on the real
    fleet have a p95 under 250 ms"**
    - A: "20 consecutive" = 20 back-to-back fresh-process runs, and p95 is
      taken over those 20.
    - B: "20 consecutive" = 20 successive turns within a session (so the fleet
      advances between samples), and p95 is a distribution over that sequence.

14. **§3 — "each not-fresh reason produces its line, with a control that makes
    the hook derive inline and fails the import-surface case."**
    - A: "the hook" that derives inline is the **stop** hook (§3's second
      paragraph), and the control is a stop-path test.
    - B: "the hook" is the prompt hook of the preceding sentence, implying the
      prompt hook can be made to derive — which contradicts "It never imports
      the derivation modules".

15. **§4 — "The producer, `reckon crew obligations`, the MCP obligations view
    and `runs.drain` all still derive. Each of the three large terms gets a
    cache keyed by the stat identity of the files it reads"**
    - A: "the three large terms" = the three numbered items in §4 (plans,
      pointers, ledger), and the sentence just says all four callers benefit.
    - B: "the three large terms" = the largest three of §1's four cold-profile
      rows — which would exclude `_plan_html.parse_meta` (3.0 s) in favour of
      the top three, leaving meta parsing unaddressed.

16. **§4 item 1 — "Target: under 0.5 s cold for 160 plans, from 19.7 s."**
    - A: "cold" = the metadata index cache is cold (a fresh process's first
      call in the same process).
    - B: "cold" = the filesystem/OS cache is cold too, a strictly harsher
      condition than A (and the condition §1's 19.7 s baseline was actually
      measured under).

17. **§4 item 1 — "rows revalidated by stat identity"**
    - A: each index row is revalidated by stat on every call (so the term is
      still O(number of plans) stats, only the HTML parse is avoided).
    - B: revalidation is itself cached/aggregated (e.g. by directory stat
      identity), so an unchanged store costs O(1).

18. **§4 item 2 — "stream observation resumes from a stored byte offset instead
    of re-parsing the whole stream."**
    - A: "stored" = persisted to disk, so a fresh process resumes too.
    - B: "stored" = held in the producer's in-memory cache across sweeps, which
      does nothing for a fresh CLI/MCP process.

19. **§4 done-when — "under 2 s cold in a fresh process and under 0.3 s on a
    repeat call, with a parity case ... on a synthesised fleet of 40 pointers
    and 200 plans."**
    - A: "a repeat call" = a second call in the same process (warm memory).
    - B: "a repeat call" = a second call after the underlying files changed and
      were revalidated (still-fresh-process but re-read), a different and
      stricter target.

20. **§4 done-when / §2 done-when / §5 — "a synthesised fleet"**
    - A: all three refer to one shared fixture (40 pointers / 200 plans per
      §4), reused across §2, §4 and §5.
    - B: each section builds its own synthesised fleet, and the §2/§5 fleets are
      unspecified in size.

21. **§5 — "a hook invocation calls none of `read_state`, `parse_events` or
    `ledger.load` (each patched to raise)."**
    - A: "calls none of" = no direct call to those functions.
    - B: "calls none of" = nothing on the transitive path reaches them (so the
      patch-to-raise catches an indirect path too), which is the stronger and
      more meaningful reading for a speed guard.

22. **§5 — "A pointer whose inputs did not change is not re-classified."**
    - A: "inputs" = the §4 item 2 set (pointer, manifest, stream size/mtime,
      review record).
    - B: "inputs" = any file the classification reads, scoped only by §4's list
      as an example.

23. **§5 — "Wall-clock budgets are recorded as measurements in the evidence,
    never asserted in the suite."** versus **§3 done-when — "20 consecutive
    cold invocations of the prompt hook on the real fleet have a p95 under
    250 ms"**
    - A: §3's p95-under-250 ms is a wall-clock budget and therefore, per §5,
      must live only in the evidence and must not be asserted in the suite —
      so §3's done-when is an evidence gate, not an automated test.
    - B: §3's p95 is a *measurement recorded in the done-when* and §5's rule
      applies only to the §5 call-count suite, so a p95 timing assertion in the
      §3 tests is permitted.

24. **§6 — "§2 and §3 land together, because a hook that reads a snapshot
    nothing writes reads nothing."** versus **§6 — "The stop path of §3 lands
    after §4 item 1"**
    - A: §3 as a whole lands with §2, except its stop path which is deferred —
      the prompt path and stop path land at different times.
    - B: §3 cannot literally "land together" with §2 if part of §3 is deferred,
      so the sentence means only the prompt path of §3 lands with §2.

---

## 5. Note on what this report is and is not

This is a restatement/read-back of the plan's intent, its two decisions and its
done-whens, followed by every sentence I found with two or more readings. It
does not propose resolutions, and items above are not defects — several are
ordinary shorthand whose intended reading is clear from context. The node's
done-when is met by this file; verifying the plan against the merged code belongs
to a separately dispatched node.

review-of-the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot — delivered report

Cited from /home/ITER/mcintos/.config/reckon/crew/reports/reckon/s22-coord/review-of-the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot-2/report.md.

# Read-back — `reckon:the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot`

Node: `review-of-the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot` (attempt 2)
Plan revision read: `8b83cfd75` (plan version 11), sections §2 (assigned) and the
whole plan for decisions and done-whens.
Role: read-back before release. This is a restatement, not a critique of the
design. Where a sentence carries two or more readings I quote it verbatim and
give each reading; I do not choose between them.

---

## 1. The plan's intent, in my own words

Every coordinator prompt runs `reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py`, and each
invocation starts a fresh Python process that re-derives the whole fleet's state
from scratch — every plan, every live run pointer, every worker event stream,
and the whole run ledger. On 2026-10-01 that cold derivation measured **31.4 s**
for the S21 session, against the hook's **30 s** limit, even after an earlier fix
had already cut 30 s out of it. The plan's premise is that no amount of tuning
the derivation reaches millisecond latency while the hook still performs the
derivation on every turn, because every term grows with the fleet (plans grow
with the number of plans, pointer classification with live pointers and stream
length, the ledger with every run ever promoted), and the limit has already been
crossed once (a fallback that added a per-pointer store-wide review scan pushed
the cold time to 64 s).

The plan therefore relocates the work. The per-project watch producer already
runs continuously and already classifies every live pointer to emit transitions;
the plan has it compute each covered session's full obligations payload and
write it as a per-session JSON snapshot (atomically, temp-file-plus-rename). The
hook stops deriving and becomes a reader: it reads the snapshot for its session
(a stat and a small JSON read), checks the snapshot is *fresh*, and either renders
the snapshot through the hook's existing formatting and injection functions or
emits a single line naming why it is not fresh and the remedy command. The prompt
path never imports the derivation modules, so a slow plan or a large stream can
no longer slow a turn. The stop hook keeps enforcing — and because a snapshot can
go stale when the producer is down or running older code, the stop hook derives
inline under a ~5 s budget, and allows the stop if the budget runs out, so no
producer state can trap a coordinator.

The plan additionally makes the derivation itself incremental (§4) so the
producer, the `reckon crew obligations` CLI, the MCP obligations view and
`runs.drain` are all cheap: each of the three large terms — plans, pointers, the
ledger — gets a cache keyed by the stat identity of the files it reads, so a
recompute reads only what changed. §5 adds call-count guards (not timing guards)
so the speed is enforced deterministically on one shared synthesised fleet, and
§6 fixes the order of work.

## 2. Every decision, restated

1. **`where-derivation-runs` — "Which process derives and publishes
   obligations?"**
   Choice: **the per-project watch producer** (`data-by="s22-coord"`,
   `data-when="2026-10-01"`), not each session's follower. Rationale as recorded:
   the producer is always on, holds warm caches across sweeps, and already does
   the per-pointer classification; a session's follower runs only while its
   harness monitor is armed, and the monitor dies/restarts on the ~25-minute
   re-arm cycle, so a follower-published snapshot would go stale exactly in the
   gap between re-arms — the hook's own follower would be the least reliable
   source for it. Context recorded: "The per-project watch producer runs
   continuously and already classifies every pointer. A session's follower runs
   only while its harness monitor is armed."
   Rejected option: `follower`.

2. **`stale-snapshot-at-stop` — "When the stop hook finds no fresh snapshot,
   what does it do?"**
   Choice: **`bounded-derive`** (`data-by="lead"`,
   `data-when="2026-10-01T13:02:22+00:00"`). The stop hook, which enforces
   obligations by refusing a stop, does not depend on a live producer: on a
   not-fresh snapshot it derives inline under a time budget of about 5 s (which
   §4 makes feasible by bringing a cold derivation under 2 s) and refuses the
   stop only if that inline result lists outstanding obligations. If the budget
   is exhausted first it allows the stop with a line naming the not-fresh reason,
   so a dead or outdated producer can never trap a coordinator. Rationale
   recorded: "Enforcement keeps working when the producer is down."
   Rejected options: `warn-and-allow` (allow the stop with a line naming the
   stale snapshot, no inline enforcement) and `block-until-producer` (refuse the
   stop until a producer is running).

## 3. Every done-when, restated

- **§1 — what the hook costs today** (`data-status="done"`): a measurement
  section, no done-when. It records the current cost — 31.4 s cold,
  11.1 s on a repeat call in the same process, 9.3 s for `s22-coord` in the same
  process, 16.9 s as the S21 session timed it under load average 43 — and the
  cold profile (plan reads `runs._project_executable_remainder` 19.7 s, pointer
  classification `recovery.classify_pointer` over 12 pointers 8.4 s (of which
  `parse_events` 4.2 s), `_plan_html.parse_meta` 3.0 s, `obligations._held_worktrees`
  2.8 s of which `crew.json` load 2.4 s).

- **§2 — the producer publishes** (`implementable`). Done when all three hold:
  (a) a pointer transition is reflected in the affected session's snapshot
  **within one producer sweep**; (b) on the **shared synthesised fleet described
  in §5, held frozen**, the snapshot the producer writes **equals** `obligations()`
  called over the same files — the parity case; and (c) a producer killed **after
  writing the temporary file but before the rename** leaves the **previous**
  snapshot intact and readable.

- **§3 — the hook reads the snapshot and never derives** (`implementable`).
  Done when tests show: (a) the prompt path's import surface excludes the three
  derivation modules (`reckon._plan_html`, `reckon._backends`, `reckon.ledger`),
  and a **deliberate mutation that makes the prompt path derive inline fails that
  test** — this is the negative control; (b) each of the three not-fresh reasons
  produces its line; (c) a pid reused by another process is not fresh; and (d)
  the stop path has **one case per branch** — fresh snapshot, inline derivation
  inside the budget, inline derivation over the budget. Separately, **recorded as
  measurement in the evidence and NOT asserted in the suite**: 20 back-to-back
  invocations of the prompt hook, each in a fresh process, on the real fleet,
  have a p95 under 250 ms.

- **§4 — the derivation becomes incremental** (`implementable`). Done when a
  parity case on the shared synthesised fleet (§5) shows the **cached** derivation
  returns the same obligations as the **uncached** one, **including after one
  plan and one pointer's stream are rewritten between calls**. Separately,
  recorded as measurement in the evidence: on the real fleet, `obligations()` for
  the largest live session takes under 2 s on the first call in a fresh process
  and under 0.3 s on a second call in the same process.

- **§5 — guards that keep it fast** (`implementable`). No literal "Done when"
  label; the section's paragraphs are its guards, and they are the done-when:
  (a) the guards run on one shared synthesised fleet — a test fixture of 40
  pointers and 200 plans under a temporary configuration home; (b) a prompt-hook
  invocation reaches **none** of `read_state`, `parse_events` or `ledger.load`,
  directly or through any callee, each patched to raise; (c) between two producer
  sweeps with one plan rewritten, `read_state` runs for that plan only; (d) a
  pointer is not re-classified unless a file its classification reads changed;
  and (e) every wall-clock budget in the plan is recorded as an evidence
  measurement, never asserted in the plan's suite.

- **§6 — order of work** (`done`): an ordering section, no done-when. It fixes
  the sequence — §4 item 1 first (largest term, independent of the snapshot,
  speeds every reader including the MCP view and CLI); then §2 and the prompt
  path of §3 together (a hook that reads a snapshot nothing writes reads
  nothing); then the stop path of §3 (after §4 item 1, because its bounded
  derivation needs that speed); then §4 items 2 and 3; each section carrying its
  own §5 guards.

## 4. Sentences with two or more readings

Quoted verbatim; readings labelled A/B. I do not choose between them.

1. **Header (lead) — "This plan moves the derivation into the process that
   already runs continuously and already watches every pointer, publishes the
   result, and makes the hook a reader of what was published."**
   - A: "This plan" is the sole subject of all three verbs — the plan moves the
     derivation, (the plan) publishes the result, and (the plan) makes the hook a
     reader.
   - B: the relative clause "that already runs continuously and already watches
     every pointer" governs a list of three verbs whose subject is *the process*
     — the process runs, publishes, and makes the hook a reader (which attributes
     the hook redesign to the producer itself, not to the plan).

2. **§2 — "Whenever one of the three triggers listed below fires, it recomputes
   the full obligations payload of every session that has a registered
   follower"**
   - A: "a registered follower" is a follower recorded in the crew/session
     registry — an attribute of the session that persists regardless of live
     traffic, so essentially every coordinating session is covered.
   - B: "a registered follower" means a follower currently attached and
     delivering (the sentence's paraphrase in the next line is "A session with no
     registered follower gets no snapshot"), so coverage tracks live follower
     traffic rather than the registry.

3. **§2 — "the one byte offset in the project's watch event stream that the
   snapshot reflects"**
   - A: there is exactly one event stream per project, and the snapshot records a
     single offset into it.
   - B: "the project's watch event stream" is the stream *family* (including
     rotation or per-consumer files); "one byte offset" identifies the position
     within whichever stream the snapshot reflects, the singular being shorthand
     for "an offset".

4. **§2 — "for every age, the instant it is measured from rather than a number
   of seconds, so a reader renders the age at read time and a snapshot never
   shows a frozen age."**
   - A: "the instant it is measured from" is an absolute wall-clock timestamp
     (UTC), so the age is `now - instant` computed by the reader.
   - B: "the instant it is measured from" is a reference the reader must interpret
     against the *snapshot's own* clock context (e.g. a monotonic/process-relative
     instant), since the reader is asserted never to see a frozen age regardless
     of clock skew.

5. **§2 — "a change to the ledger or to the plan store, read from the stat
   identity of `docs/state/<project>/` and `docs/plans/`"**
   - A: the trigger watches the stat identity of those two **directories** (their
     mtime/ino), so an in-place edit of a plan file that does not touch the
     directory's own mtime may not fire this trigger.
   - B: "the stat identity of `docs/state/<project>/` and `docs/plans/`" is the
     aggregate stat identity of the contents under those paths, so any file added,
     removed or modified in them, and any revalidation stat the §4 caches use,
     is what decides the trigger.

6. **§2 — "a floor tick every 60 s, so obligations that become due by the
   passage of time, such as a stale promotable run, appear without any other
   event."**
   - A: the floor tick fires per producer per project every 60 s, recomputing the
     full payload as the surrounding sentence says all three triggers do.
   - B: "every 60 s" is a global cadence across the whole producer rather than one
     tick per project, so with several projects covered the effective per-project
     period is longer than 60 s.

7. **§2 done-when — "on the shared synthesised fleet described in §5, held
   frozen, the snapshot the producer writes equals `obligations()` called over
   the same files (the parity case)"**
   - A: "held frozen" modifies the *fleet* — the fixture is not mutated during the
     comparison, so both readers see one fixed state.
   - B: "held frozen" modifies the *producer* — the producer is paused/stopped at
     a known point so the snapshot on disk and the live `obligations()` call are
     provably from the same state, which is a different test mechanism.

8. **§2 done-when — "a pointer transition is reflected in the session's snapshot
   within one producer sweep"**
   - A: "within one producer sweep" is the producer's own sweep of its event
     source, so the bound is "the next time the producer runs", however long that
     is under load.
   - B: "within one producer sweep" is a bounded latency (the sweep period), i.e.
     a timing bound the test must honour, not merely "eventually by the next
     sweep".

9. **§3 — "its code stamp equals the installed source's"**
   - A: "the installed source's stamp" is the stamp of the code the *hook process
     itself is running* (so a hook in a worktree compares to that worktree's
     stamp), matching whatever `code_stamp` the watcher reports.
   - B: "the installed source's stamp" is the stamp of the code at the canonical
     installed location (the shared/root environment), independent of which tree
     the hook process resolved from — the two differ whenever a worktree repoints
     the shared environment.

10. **§3 — "A snapshot that is not fresh is replaced by one line naming the
    reason (no producer, producer running older code, or the age of the
    snapshot) and the remedy, `reckon crew watch --ensure --project
    <project>`."**
    - A: "replaced by one line" = the hook **emits** a single line in place of the
      rendered obligations (the snapshot payload the hook injects is substituted
      with one line of text); the snapshot file is untouched.
    - B: "replaced by one line" = the hook **rewrites the snapshot** so its
      contents become the one-line message (a file mutation), which a
      read-only hook path would not do.

11. **§3 — "The prompt path never imports the derivation modules
    (`reckon._plan_html`, `reckon._backends`, `reckon.ledger` and anything that
    imports them), so a slow plan or a large stream can no longer slow a turn."**
    - A: "and anything that imports them" is the *transitive closure rule* — the
      prompt path must not import any module that (transitively) imports the three,
      which is the constraint the done-when's import-surface test checks.
    - B: "and anything that imports them" is part of the *definition of the
      forbidden set* — the three named modules plus any module that imports them
      are all forbidden to import, a larger named set than the three.

12. **§3 — "it imports the derivation lazily and derives inline under a budget of
    about 5 s"**
    - A: the budget is a fixed constant on the order of 5 s, and the stop path
      hardcodes it.
    - B: "about 5 s" is a default that must be overridable, because the §3
      done-when requires a case "inline derivation over the budget", which cannot
      deterministically be produced by waiting out a real ~5 s budget.

13. **§3 done-when — "a deliberate mutation that makes the prompt path derive
    inline fails that test (the negative control)"**
    - A: "derive inline" means the prompt path imports and calls the derivation
      modules (the import-surface exclusion is what must break), so the mutation
      is applied to the prompt path's imports.
    - B: "derive inline" means the prompt path takes the stop-path's inline
      derivation route (a behavioural fallthrough), so the mutation is applied to
      the freshness/read logic, and the import-surface test is only one of the
      things that must fail.

14. **§3 — "Recorded as measurement in the evidence, not asserted in the suite:
    20 back-to-back invocations of the prompt hook, each in a fresh process on
    the real fleet, have a p95 under 250 ms."**
    - A: "20 back-to-back invocations" = 20 consecutive fresh-process runs on a
      static real fleet, with p95 taken over those 20 samples (for n=20, p95 is
      effectively between the 19th and 20th order statistic — near the maximum).
    - B: "20 back-to-back invocations" = 20 successive turns within one session
      (so the fleet advances between samples), and "p95" is read as a loose
      "the slow tail stays under", i.e. effectively a max rather than a strict
      percentile.

15. **§5 — "Between two producer sweeps with one plan rewritten, `read_state`
    runs for that plan only."**
    - A: "for that plan only" bounds the invoked *plan identity* — `read_state`
      may be called more than once, but only ever for the one rewritten plan, and
      never for any unchanged plan.
    - B: "for that plan only" bounds the *call count* — exactly one `read_state`
      call total across the interval, for the rewritten plan.

16. **§4 — "Each of the three items below (plans, pointers, the ledger) gets a
    cache keyed by the stat identity of the files it reads, so a recompute only
    reads what changed."**
    - A: "only reads what changed" is literal — a recompute touches (reads) only
      the files whose stat identity moved.
    - B: "only reads what changed" is figurative — a recompute still **stats**
      every file (that is how stat identity is learned), and only the *expensive
      parse* is confined to the changed files, so "reads" excludes the revalidation
      stats.

17. **§4 item 1 — "Target: under 0.5 s in a fresh process for 160 plans, from
    19.7 s for the plan reads plus 3.0 s for meta parsing."**
    - A: the 0.5 s target covers the whole replaced term — plan state reads **and**
      meta parsing — so the baseline it replaces is the 22.7 s sum.
    - B: the 0.5 s target covers only the plan-read sub-term (the sentence names it
      "for 160 plans" and cites 19.7 s "for the plan reads"), leaving meta parsing
      in scope only for §4's separate "meta fields `parse_meta` extracts come from
      the same rows".

18. **§4 item 2 — "keyed by the stat identity of every file the classification
    reads (at least the pointer, manifest, stream and review record)."**
    - A: "at least" warns that the enumerated set is non-exhaustive, and the
      implementation's completeness (any other file it reads is also in the key)
      is what §5's "a file its classification reads changed" then tests.
    - B: "at least" enumerates the *minimum* the key must carry, so an
      implementation keyed on exactly those four is acceptable and additional files
      are optional.

19. **§4 item 3 — "This should reuse the per-run ledger files from
    the-ledger-is-one-file-per-run where they exist, not build a second index."**
    - A: "where they exist" is a hard conditional — the reuse is required only for
      runs that already have per-run files, and a fallback to `crew.json` for the
      rest is in scope.
    - B: "where they exist" presupposes they exist (the referenced plan has
      landed), so reuse is required, and the fallback is out of scope — the
      "should" is a soft word over a firm requirement.

20. **§4 item 3 — "`_held_worktrees` reads the rows it needs rather than loading
    `crew.json` whole."**
    - A: it avoids reading `crew.json` at all (reads only the per-run ledger
      files), which is what "rather than loading `crew.json` whole" implies.
    - B: it may still open `crew.json` but materialise only the rows it needs
      (streamed/partial read), so the claim is about memory/parse cost, not about
      never touching the file.

21. **§4 done-when — "including after one plan and one pointer's stream are
    rewritten between calls."**
    - A: "rewritten" means the two files' **contents** change (a real plan edit and
      appended stream bytes), so the cache's stat-identity key must notice content
      change.
    - B: "rewritten" means the files are **replaced/rewritten with identical stat
      identity** (same size and mtime granularity), i.e. a deliberate
      false-negative probe of the stat-identity scheme; the two demand opposite
      things of the implementation.

22. **§5 — "Every wall-clock budget in this plan is recorded as a measurement in
    the evidence and never asserted in the suite."** versus **§4 item 1 —
    "Target: under 0.5 s in a fresh process for 160 plans"** and **§3 —
    "a budget of about 5 s"**
    - A: the "0.5 s" and "about 5 s" figures are wall-clock budgets like the p95,
      and §5 forbids all of them from the suite — so the 0.5 s "Target" is
      evidence-only and the ~5 s budget is a production constant, not a test
      bound.
    - B: only the figures explicitly called "recorded as measurement" (the p95,
      the §4 2 s/0.3 s pair) are evidence-only; §4's "Target:" is a suite-asserted
      gate, and the ~5 s budget is exercised by the over-budget test case.

23. **Decision `stale-snapshot-at-stop` — "the stop hook derives inline under a
    budget of about 5 s and blocks only on that result."**
    - A: "blocks only on that result" = the block/allow decision is taken
      *solely* from the inline derivation's result, never from any other signal
      (no snapshot, no producer state).
    - B: "blocks only on that result" = the stop is refused *only when* that
      result lists outstanding obligations (the ordinary "block on true, allow
      otherwise"), a weaker claim.

24. **§6 — "§2 and the prompt path of §3 land together, because a hook that
    reads a snapshot nothing writes reads nothing."**
    - A: "§2 and the prompt path of §3 land together" is a hard co-landing
      requirement — neither may merge without the other.
    - B: it states the *reason* they are sequenced adjacently (so ordering suffices
      and a brief window where one has landed and the other has not is tolerable),
      the "together" being intent rather than an atomic-merge requirement.

## 5. What this report is and is not

This is a restatement/read-back of the plan's intent, its two decisions and its
done-whens, followed by every sentence I found with two or more readings, quoted
verbatim. It does not propose resolutions, and the items above are not defects —
several are ordinary shorthand whose intended reading is clear from context. No
plan or index state is edited here; the coordinator lands any writeback. The
node's done-when is met by this file; verifying the released plan against merged
code belongs to a separately dispatched test node.

The stop hook now re-applies the acknowledgements in force (_acknowledgements_in_force and _partition_acknowledged) to a fresh snapshot's list before refusing, so an ack written after the snapshot was computed is honoured at once, and an unreadable-review-record finding joined _NON_BLOCKING_KINDS so it prompts without holding a stop open; base arm "2 failed, 45 passed in 14.71s" against head arm "47 passed in 16.83s".

<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>

<title>Snapshot tests under load — failure investigation</title>
<style>
 body { font-family: system-ui, sans-serif; max-width: 62rem; margin: 2rem auto; padding: 0 1rem; line-height: 1.5; }
 table { border-collapse: collapse; width: 100%; margin: 1rem 0; }

 .cleared { color: #146c2e; font-weight: 600; }
 .open { color: #9a3412; font-weight: 600; }
</style>
</head>
<body>
<h1>Snapshot tests under load — why two ids fail in the suite and pass alone</h1>
<p>
Node <code>snapshot-tests-under-load</code> (role: test — attribution, not verdict), run
<code>r-20261004T011824432285-snapshot-tests-under-load</code>, 2026-10-04.
Subject: <code>tests/test_obligations_snapshot_is_published.py</code> at the worktree
(HEAD <code>78a3735b7</code>) and at <code>29e9c94ca</code>, the parent of the merge that added the
<code>joined_watch_sweeps</code> fixture. Both ids failed in the half-1 suite run at
<code>76ed64992</code> under <code>pytest -n 8</code>
(<code>~/.config/reckon/crew/runs/r-20261004T001205658772-the-integrated-suite-at-76ed64992-half-1/suite-half-1-worktree.log</code>):
</p>
<ul>
<li><code>test_a_file_change_and_the_floor_tick_each_republish</code> — line 368,
<code>assert sweep_at(_computed_at(first) + timedelta(seconds=5)) == []</code>, returned
<code>[PosixPath('…/test_a_file_change_and_the_flo0/config/crew/obligations/snapshot-fixture/coordinator-fixture.json')]</code>.</li>
<li><code>test_a_slow_sweep_does_not_hold_the_next_transition</code> — line 548,
<code>assert started.wait(timeout=30.0)</code> was <code>False</code>: the claim's first sweep never called
the test's patched <code>sweep</code>. Both failures were on <code>[gw2]</code>, ~10 s apart.</li>
</ul>

<h2>Measurement — 30 loops per revision under load</h2>
<p>
Each loop runs the test file under <code>pytest -n 8</code> with a per-loop temporary
<code>RECKON_HOME</code> and <code>--basetemp</code> under the run's scratch, beside 8 CPU spinner
processes (python busy loops, killed by explicit PID). Logs: one file per loop, exit code appended.
</p>
<table>
<tr><th>id</th><th>main <code>78a3735b7</code> (30 loops)</th><th><code>29e9c94ca</code> (30 loops)</th><th>instrumented main (45 loops, <code>-p sweeplog</code>)</th></tr>
<tr><td><code>test_a_file_change_and_the_floor_tick_each_republish</code></td><td>0 failures</td><td>0 failures</td><td>0 failures</td></tr>
<tr><td><code>test_a_slow_sweep_does_not_hold_the_next_transition</code></td><td>0 failures</td><td>0 failures</td><td>0 failures</td></tr>
<tr><td>whole file (12 tests per loop)</td><td>30/30 loops exit 0</td><td>30/30 loops exit 0</td><td>45/45 loops exit 0</td></tr>
</table>
<p>
Loop logs: <code>loops/main/loop-01..30.log</code>, <code>loops/base/loop-01..30.log</code>,
<code>loops/inst-main/loop-NN.log</code>; per-loop exit/second/summary/failed-id rows in each
directory's <code>progress.txt</code>; the arms' trees and head shas in each <code>header.txt</code>
(main = worktree HEAD; base = extraction of <code>29e9c94ca</code> at
<code>&lt;scratch&gt;/tree-base</code>). Loop wall time 6–17 s. The test file is byte-identical across
the three revisions (<code>git archive</code> extraction checked by direct comparison).
</p>

<h2>Verdict on the declared negative control</h2>
<p>
The declared control: a loop count of zero failures at the parent beside a nonzero count at main
implicates <code>joined_watch_sweeps</code>; equal counts clear it. Measured: <strong>0/30 at main,
0/30 at the parent — equal counts, and the fixture is cleared by count.</strong>
The fixture is also cleared by mechanism: it never creates, clears or keys
<code>obligation_snapshot._SWEEPS</code>; it joins by thread name with a bounded
<code>WATCH_SWEEP_JOIN_SECONDS</code>, and the instrumented traces show every sweep thread exiting in
≤ 1.2 s under load. It is the messenger that surfaces the state, not the state's author.
</p>

<h2>What the trace shows about the failed assertion (id 1)</h2>
<p>
The instrumented arm (<code>loops/inst-main/trace-NN.txt</code>) logs every <code>sweep()</code> call
with its <code>now</code>, the cadence memory before and after, and the write count. One MainThread
sequence (trace-41, one worker's <code>test_a_file_change_and_the_floor_tick_each_republish</code>):
</p>
<pre>
producer write  now=01:38:34.600161  before[none]  after[checked_at=34.600161 published_at=34.600161 identity=0]  nwritten=1
sweep_at(C+5)   now=01:38:39.600161  before[checked_at=34.600161 …]  after[unchanged]  nwritten=0   -> []          (assert passes)
sweep_at(C+15)  now=01:38:49.600161  before[checked_at=34.600161 …]  after[checked_at=49.600161 identity=1]  nwritten=1   (plan file written; identity 0 -> 1)
sweep_at(C+61)  now=01:39:50.600161  before[checked_at=49.600161 …]  after[checked_at=50.600161 identity=1]  nwritten=1   (floor tick)
</pre>
<p>
The +5 call lands with margin <code>now − checked_at = 5.000 s</code> against
<code>STAT_IDENTITY_INTERVAL_SECONDS = 10.0</code>: the cached branch runs, the just-written plan
file is deliberately <em>not</em> looked for yet, <code>nwritten=0</code>, and the assert's
<code>[]</code> follows — exactly as the test's comment says it should.
</p>
<p>
<code>obligation_snapshot.sweep()</code> takes <strong>one</strong> instant per call
(<code>instant = _utc_now() if now is None else now</code>) and stamps it into three places: the
document (<code>document_for(…, computed_at=instant)</code>), and the memory's
<code>checked_at</code> and <code>published_at</code> on the write path. A cached call preserves
both memory fields untouched. Therefore, given a single key and a single memory, at
<code>now = C + 5</code>:
</p>
<ul>
<li><code>checked</code> is unconditionally true (margin ≤ 5 &lt; 10, because
<code>checked_at ≥ published_at = C</code>);</li>
<li>with <code>checked</code> true, <code>identity</code> <em>is</em> <code>memory.identity</code>,
so the identity trigger cannot fire;</li>
<li>the floor needs <code>now − published_at ≥ 60</code>, i.e. <code>published_at ≤ C − 55</code>,
which a memory whose <code>published_at</code> is <code>C</code> cannot satisfy.</li>
</ul>
<p>
<strong>The assert is arithmetically safe whenever the memory the test consults is the memory the
producer's sweep wrote.</strong> The observed failure (a republish returned) therefore requires the
consulted memory to be absent or stale relative to the writer of <code>first</code> — a different
process, a different home resolution, or a second module instance holding its own
<code>_SWEEPS</code>. Two candidate mechanisms were tested and falsified; the divergence is not
reproducible in 105 loop runs of this file alone.
</p>

<h2>Hypotheses tested</h2>
<table>
<tr><th>Hypothesis</th><th>Test</th><th>Result</th></tr>
<tr>
<td>SPEC hypothesis: a sweep thread whose bounded join timed out keeps a <code>sweep_lock</code> a later producer shares.</td>
<td>Read <code>runs._publish_watch_stream</code> / <code>_run_obligation_sweep</code> / <code>join_watch_sweeps</code>; instrumented trace of every SWEEPTHREAD ENTER/EXIT under load.</td>
<td><span class="open">Not supported by any measurement.</span> The lock is released in a <code>finally</code>; a later claim builds a fresh producer with a fresh lock; ENTER→EXIT ≤ 1.2 s in all 45 instrumented loops; no 30 s stall appears. Formally still open only because the failure itself never reproduced here.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Module-identity split: the by-path loader in <code>reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.snapshot_module()</code> (and <code>cli.py</code> ~2984) replaces <code>sys.modules["reckon.crew.obligation_snapshot"]</code> with a second instance, giving the producer and the test different <code>_SWEEPS</code> and different patched <code>sweep</code> objects.</td>
<td>Three probes in the repo's own venv: <code>scripts/probe_bypath.py</code> run before the test file in one pytest process (<code>loops/split-repro.log</code>); <code>scripts/ordering_probe.py</code> / <code>ordering_probe_b.py</code> (<code>loops/instance-probes.log</code>), covering both orderings of canonical import vs by-path load.</td>
<td><span class="cleared">Falsified.</span> The by-path load does build a second instance and does replace the <code>sys.modules</code> entry, but <code>from reckon.crew import obligation_snapshot</code> resolves the parent-module attribute in every ordering — producer and test always hold the same instance and share one <code>_SWEEPS</code> (probe A: producer is test instance = true, even with <code>sys.modules</code> holding the by-path copy; probe B: both are the by-path copy). Consistently, all 12 tests in the file passed in the probe run (1 failed = the probe's own expected-split assertion, 12 passed).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>joined_watch_sweeps</code> fixture implicated.</td>
<td>The 30/30 vs 30/30 loop arms (declared negative control).</td>
<td><span class="cleared">Cleared by count</span> (equal counts) and by mechanism (above).</td>
</tr>
</table>

<h2>What remains open</h2>
<p>
The failing branch needs <code>memory is None</code> (or a stale memory) at the test's direct
<code>sweep()</code> call: at <code>sweep_at(C+5)</code>, a missing memory makes <code>due</code>
true (the first sweep of a producer always writes), which returns the written path and fails the
assert exactly as the log shows. A missing/stale memory cannot arise within one process, one
module instance and one <code>RECKON_HOME</code> (the home is set per test by
<code>monkeypatch.setenv</code> and resolved per call). Every single-file loop I could build —
with load, with xdist, with instrumentation — stays within that envelope, which is why 105 runs
show zero failures. The original failure ran the <em>whole suite</em>: a worker there also carries
the hook/watch tests, and with <code>--dist load</code> the per-worker file mix and import order
are scheduling-dependent. The one structural difference I can name between the failing context and
my loops is that context, and it is exactly where a second instance or a stale key could be born;
it is not reachable from this file alone. The honest statement is: <strong>the trigger is a
consulted-memory divergence, its required arithmetic is proven, the fixture is cleared, the module
split is falsified, and the remaining reproduction demands the full-suite worker context.</strong>
</p>

<h2>Proposed fixing node</h2>
<p>Two phases; the first makes the second cheap.</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Make the failure self-diagnosing (write paths:
<code>tests/test_obligations_snapshot_is_published.py</code>, and optionally a read-only accessor in
<code>reckon/crew/obligation_snapshot.py</code>).</strong> At both asserts, capture on failure: the
key <code>str(snapshot_dir(project))</code>, the <code>_SWEEPS</code> entry for that key
(<code>checked_at</code>, <code>published_at</code>, identity length), the resolved
<code>RECKON_HOME</code>, the current thread names, and whether <code>sys.modules</code>' entry for
<code>reckon.crew.obligation_snapshot</code> differs from the test module's instance.
Done-when: the assert failure message alone names which divergence occurred, and the existing
passing behaviour is unchanged (30 loops, 0 new failures).</li>
<li><strong>Reproduce in the true context and fix the divergence (write path: whichever file the
capture names — the loader in <code>reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py</code>, or the memory
keying in <code>reckon/crew/obligation_snapshot.py</code>).</strong> Run the snapshot file in a
worker that also runs the hook/watch tests (<code>pytest -n 8</code> over a file set that includes
both), ≥ 30 iterations under the same CPU load generator. Done-when: either the instrumented
failure fires and names the divergence, or 30 iterations pass in that context; if the by-path
loader is named, the fix is to make the loader return the canonical instance rather than replace
the <code>sys.modules</code> entry.</li>
</ol>

§21 — A worktree-held row is rechecked when the hook reads it

reckon-build · 2026-10-04T07:41:06.096Z

docs/evidence/fragments/the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot/a-worktree-held-row-is-rechecked-at-read.html — the prompt and stop paths recheck each worktree-held row where it is offered, resolving the run from its own per-run ledger file alone (never the aggregate beside it) and the tree from the path that record names, and drop the row when the record shows the tree released or that tree is no longer a directory; recorded in that fragment.

reckon-build · 2026-10-04T07:47:12.612Z

an independent review of run r-20261004T071744562101-a-worktree-held-row-is-rechecked-at-read is stored at both review paths — total 96/100 (goal_fidelity 19, evidence 19, scope_discipline 20, durability 19, fit 19), reviewed_base_sha e1e9c9c35, reviewed_head_sha c694d0ce, added_failure_count 0 with no added ids — and the change is judged sound: the hook rechecks each worktree-held row at read time in both entry points and drops it when the run's per-run record or the tree on disk says the tree is gone.

the-producer-publishes-the-snapshot (head 7790fdcba, review 90, 0 added) is held unmerged because merging it deploys into the live producer, which re-execs on new code. Read-only measurements against the real fleet at 06:15Z found three costs.

  • The sweep publishes for all 50 follower registrations in reckon's watch directory, and only 3 are live: s21-fin, s22-coord and s22n-reckon. The rest date back to mid-September.
  • Each trigger runs obligations(project, session) once per session, a few seconds each, inside the producer's own loop. Every 60 s floor tick would therefore spend minutes blocking transitions for every follower.
  • The per-file stat trigger walks 1,424 files under docs/state/reckon and docs/plans in 0.12 to 0.55 s, on every one-second poll.

§2 now names live registrations, and the repair node snapshot-sweep-is-bounded lands three bounds on top of 7790fdcba. First, only live followers are published. Second, the fleet state is derived once per sweep and sliced per session, rather than derived once per session. Third, the stat trigger is evaluated at most every 10 s and skips docs/state/<project>/runs/, whose changes arrive as pointer transitions anyway. The node also carries the review's two follow-ons: the zombie check that _process_is_running exists for but is never called, and a stray token in a comment.

s22-coord · 2026-10-03

A snapshot computed 9 seconds before the ack (computed_at 02:00:13Z, ack at 02:00:22Z) went on listing the acknowledged obligation. The stop hook trusts any snapshot under 120 s old, so it blocked on an obligation already deferred until the next sweep, or until the snapshot aged past freshness. Done when an ack, like a transition, triggers the session's republish, or when the stop hook re-applies _acknowledgements_in_force to a fresh snapshot's list before refusing.

§17 landed on 2026-10-04, merged at 222b6706f. join_watch_sweeps in reckon/crew/runs.py joins every producer's in-flight sweep with a bound and clears the registry, and the autouse joined_watch_sweeps fixture calls it after each test. The first head passed its focused population but errored in the whole suite: its own test left a process-wide json.loads forgery installed through teardown, and the per-test reap decoded a retained seat record under it. The repair scopes every forgery to the test body and adds a regression that writes the seat record into the test's own tree.

Measure: stacked whole suite, main 6f63782e4 against its merge with head 12237f0cb, -n 10. Obligation-sweep thread exceptions went from 16 to 0, and no failure is attributable to the change. The one added id was test_producer_takes_new_code.py::test_the_follow_json_surface_carries_the_stale_producer, a crashed xdist worker that passes 3 of 3 on both trees, filed under a-follower-outlives-its-monitor §12. A focused gate on the merged code ran 40 passed. Reviews scored 95 and then 97. The seat-record mirror defect found in review is §18.

The first node of §20 (snapshot-tests-under-load, 2026-10-04) is recorded at docs/evidence/fragments/the-obligations-hook-reads-a-snapshot/snapshot-tests-under-load.html.

  • No reproduction. The file looped under -n 8 beside eight CPU spinners failed 0 of 30 times at main 78a3735b7 and 0 of 30 times at 29e9c94ca, the parent before the §17 join fixture. An instrumented arm failed 0 of 45 times.
  • The fixture is cleared, by equal counts and by mechanism. It never touches obligation_snapshot._SWEEPS, and every sweep thread exited within 1.2 s under load.
  • Two hypotheses are falsified: a sweep_lock kept by a join that timed out, and a second module instance from the by-path hook loader.
  • The failing branch is proven arithmetically. It needs the memory consulted at the test's direct sweep() call to be missing or stale relative to the producer's write. That cannot happen within one process, one module instance and one RECKON_HOME, so the reproduction needs the full-suite worker context.

Next node: make both asserts self-diagnosing, so that a failure names the cache key, the cached entry, the resolved home, the live threads and whether sys.modules holds a different instance. Then reproduce in the full-suite context, or wait for the next occurrence to name its own cause.

Correction to the comment above. It said every sweep thread exited within 1.2 s under load. The investigation's own traces, as measured by its review (score 85), hold 47 of 360 exits above 1.2 s, with a maximum of 2.978 s. The evidence fragment is corrected and carries a note saying so. The conclusion is unchanged: 2.978 s is well inside the 10 s join bound and the 30 s wait the failed test used, so no join timed out and no sweep_lock was left held.

On 2026-10-04 the self-diagnosing asserts landed at e2958eaa9. Both snapshot assertions now name, on failure, the key, its _SWEEPS entry or its absence, RECKON_HOME, the live threads and any second module instance. The review scored 96. One of the two diagnostics, on the 30 s started.wait, is not yet shown to fire.

A context reproduction followed. Both ids failed together once, in a coordinator head arm of 130 follower and re-arm files at -n 8: main e41b7eecc merged with a-re-attach-replays-the-gap, before the diagnostics landed. Neither failed in the matching base arm, and both pass 3 of 3 alone on both trees. That fits the investigation's conclusion that the trigger needs other tests sharing the worker, and follower tests are a candidate. The fix waits on the next occurrence naming its divergence.

unknown · undated

A further reproduction, 2026-10-04: both snapshot ids failed together again in a coordinator base arm. That arm was the 130 follower files at -n 8 on main e41b7eecc, with no follower change applied. So the pair is a property of main under that worker mix, not of any change being measured.

unknown · undated

Cause found, 2026-10-04. The self-diagnosing asserts fired in a coordinator whole-suite head arm (stacked at main 61abcb5df, -n 10, worker gw2). Both failures reported the same three things:

  • sys.modules holds a different instance of reckon.crew.obligation_snapshot from the one the test imported;
  • that other instance's _SWEEPS held the producer's entry;
  • the test's own instance had none ("absent").

Both by-path loaders assign sys.modules["reckon.crew.obligation_snapshot"] unconditionally: snapshot_module() in reckon/hooks/coordinator_obligations.py, and the loader near reckon/cli.py:3012. So a test that runs either one after the module is already imported splits the process. Producer code reaches the by-path copy through sys.modules, while the test holds the package attribute. The investigation's isolated probes could not reproduce this, because the split needs an earlier test in the same worker to run a loader. The fix node, the-snapshot-loader-keeps-one-instance, makes both loaders reuse an instance already loaded.

unknown · undated

§20 landed on 2026-10-04, merged at ad049341f.

Cause. The hook's snapshot_module() assigned sys.modules["reckon.crew.obligation_snapshot"] on every call. A worker that first imported reckon.cli, whose loader registers the module, and then ran a hook test ended up with two instances. The producer published its sweep memory on one while the snapshot test read the other.

Fix. The loader returns an instance sys.modules already holds, and loads by path, still without the crew facade, only when none is registered. The cli loader already did this.

Path to it. Four nodes: a load investigation that cleared the §17 fixture and ruled out two hypotheses; self-diagnosing asserts; a whole-suite run in which those asserts named the split; and this fix. An identity test and a serial reproduction both fail at base and pass at head. The 11-file obligations and hook population ran 138 passed at both. Review scored 95.

unknown · undated

§19 landed on 2026-10-04, merged at 01e44c2c0. When a promotable run's only recorded commit equals its dispatch base, the recovery classifier's next action, and so the hook's, offers crew complete --gate not-run --no-commit '<why>' --outcome '<what>' instead of citing the base. A run with commits beyond its base keeps its --commit list. A test takes the offered command, fills only its placeholders and promotes, while the base revision's command is refused.

Measured stacked at main 9a641736e on 34 next-action files: 675 passed at base and head, with no added failure. Review scored 98.

Follow-on: a promotable run whose recorded commit resolves to nothing, for example after a reclaimed worktree, still gets the plain --gate <verdict> offer with neither --commit nor --no-commit, and promotion refuses that.

§18 landed on 2026-10-04, merged at d52daaee8. A mirrored seat record carries the home it was written under, so the session-end reap matches a live producer whose test tree has been pruned. The mirror's set-aside merges instead of replacing, so the reap sees every test's records. A stub-producer test fails at base and passes at head.

Measured with a stacked whole suite at main 0f5941a18: 5 failures at base and 7 at head, with no crashed worker and no out-of-space errors. The three added ids are reap-child timing cases that pass 3 of 3 alone on both trees, and two of them failed in an earlier base arm. Review scored 96.

§21 landed on 2026-10-04, merged at ee407e9d8. A worktree-held row is rechecked at read time, and dropped when the run's per-run record shows its worktree released or the tree is no longer a directory. The first head fell back to parsing the 81 MB aggregate crew.json (0.8 s) when a per-run file was missing; that fallback was removed before review, so a missing record is no evidence and the tree stat decides.
